Alexandre Ghiti

Patches Submitted (2)
Last comment 2026-10-01 [PATCH] mm/swap: submit the last readahead batch before unplugging Neutral LLM

The patch addresses an issue where the last readahead batch is not submitted before unplugging, which can lead to unexpected behavior in block I/O operations. The fix involves moving the submission of the final batch before finishing the plug, ensuring that all batches are properly handled.

2 reviewer comments — 0 participants
0 participants
Joshua Hahn Reviewed-by Neutral LLM
Found the patch reasonable and suitable for review. Raised a question about the need for the Fixes: tag, which Andrew Morton later clarified.
Show original comment 
On Thu,  1 Oct 2026 10:57:12 +0200 Alexandre Ghiti <alex@ghiti.fr> wrote:

> Since block swap I/O is batched in struct swap_iocb, swap_add_folio()
> only appends a folio to the pending batch when its slot directly follows
> the previous one, and submits the pending batch otherwise. Batches
> submitted inside the readahead loop go through the plug, where the block
> layer can still merge them, but the final batch is submitted after
> blk_finish_plug() and always becomes a separate request.
> 
> So submit the final batch before finishing the plug.

Hi Alex,

I read through the code and this looks very reasonable to me.

Andrew, what do we usually do in these cases? I am wondering if it will
just become folded into the original commit or if this should still be
standalone.

Thank you both! Have a great day : -)
Joshua

Signals: INFO
Last comment 2026-09-28 [RFC PATCH 0/4] mm/zswap: make shrinker writeback work with iocost 4 patches
Show 4 individual patches
  • [1/4] block: do not issue background swap bios as root Neutral Last comment 2026-09-28 Christoph Hellwig
  • [2/4] mm/zswap: turn shrink_memcg_cb()'s argument into a flags word
  • [3/4] mm/zswap: allow writeback to be throttled by the cgroup IO controllers
  • [4/4] mm/zswap: move reclaim-driven writeback to a kworker
Neutral LLM

This RFC patch series aims to improve the performance of zswap by making its shrinker writeback work with iocost. The current implementation causes a large production workload to be slower when zswap is enabled, due to two main issues: (1) zswap writeback being charged as debt and causing stalls in application allocations, and (2) throttled writes sleeping in their submitter, which can be an application thread. The patches propose to throttle zswap writeback instead of making it REQ_SWAP, and move the reclaim-driven writeback to a per-lruvec kworker.

1 reviewer comment — 0 participants
0 participants
Christoph Hellwig ↳ replying to Alexandre Ghiti Needs Work LLM
Raised concerns about the implications of using REQ_BACKGROUND for throttled I/O. Suggested that the caller should be able to tolerate a failure, implying that foreground paging is not suitable.
Show original comment 
On Mon, Sep 28, 2026 at 10:18:36AM +0200, Alexandre Ghiti wrote:
> bio_issue_as_root_blkg() issues every REQ_SWAP bio as root, bypassing
> the cgroup IO controllers, because reclaim cannot stall indefinitely on
> swap writes. Some swap writes are not urgent though: zswap writeback is
> best effort.
> 
> Such a bio cannot simply drop REQ_SWAP: dm-crypt and dm-thin rely on it
> for limit_swap_bios, which caps in-flight swap bios to avoid a memory
> deadlock, see commit a666e5c05e7c ("dm: fix deadlock when swapping to
> encrypted device").

Is that true?  REQ_BACKGROUND very much implies the I/O can the throttled
and thus better not be foreground paging, and thus the caller should be
able to tolerate a failure.

Patches Submitted (13)
Last comment 2026-09-29 [PATCH 0/6] btrfs: allocator perf improvements Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch series improves the performance of the Btrfs allocator by reducing unnecessary operations on large file systems. Specifically, it optimizes the allocation process to skip unhelpful size class relaxing loops and redundant checks in the find_free_extent function. This results in significant speedup for certain workloads, such as running 20k fallocates in a loop on a sparse file system, from ~40s to ~7s.

3 reviewer comments — 0 participants
0 participants
Sun YangKai ↳ replying to Boris Burkov Reviewed-by Needs Work LLM
reviewer suggested using a more efficient method, such as sorting block groups by free space, to improve the lookup process in allocation
Show original comment 
Hi Boris,

Glad to see this patchset resolves the long existing blockgroup 
allocator performance issue and I'm really impressed by the numbers of 
performance improvement.

IMO we currently go along the list to look for a proper bg that have 
enough free space, which means we may have to go through thousands of 
bgs during the allocation, I wonder if it is worthy to use a more 
efficient method like something sorted by bg's free space to improve the 
lookup process?

Please correct me if I got anything wrong.

And thanks for bringing these great patches.

Sun Yangkai
View on lore ↗
Signals: wondered if current approach is worthy, suggested alternative method
Boris Burkov (author) ↳ replying to Sun YangKai Neutral LLM
Author Boris Burkov addressed a concern about implementing gap size indexing at the block group level, explaining that it would introduce contention due to a write lock on the global ordering of block groups. He suggested alternative solutions such as batching or queuing work to update the ordering, and expressed interest in considering Martin's patch for scalability improvements.
Show original comment 
I considered this while working on the size class stuff some years ago.
If you look into the free space cache inside each block group, it does
in fact index by gap size as well as gap offset.

However if you do that at the block group level, then each allocation
requires a write to the global ordering of block groups so that means
having many parallel allocating threads now requires them to fight over
a write lock on that ordered structure. I think that at least rules out
naive solutions.

You could probably resolve it with some more clever scheme like batching
or queuing the work to update the ordering, or maybe some duplicated
view with atomic cut overs.

If you are interested in allocator performance, another thing we should
consider is resurrecting more of
https://lore.kernel.org/linux-btrfs/0102019bb2ff5805-8aa151b8-1fe7-4087-9610-4c3314b3b144-000000@eu-west-1.amazonses.com/

I actually didn't realize that Martin had hit a few of the same things
in that series so now I feel a bit bad about not working harder to
incorporate his work earlier.

If this series of mine goes in, we are ok on the put/get part since I
just got rid of it, and I was planning to get rid of data_rwsem after
https://lore.kernel.org/linux-btrfs/20260916-btrfs-remove-v1-space-cache-v3-0-10ebde03b96f@columbia.edu/
goes in. But I have not tried to tackle the scalability of groups_sem
yet, and would probably start by seriously evaluating Martin's patch and
re-sending his work for that one.
View on lore ↗
Signals: considered this while working on size class stuff some years ago, I think that at least rules out naive solutions
Martin Raiber ↳ replying to Boris Burkov Reviewed-by Needs Work LLM
reviewer questioned the suitability of replacing the current locking mechanism with a percpu semaphore, suggesting potential performance issues and proposing alternative solutions such as RCU lists or arenas like memory allocators
Show original comment 
Np, I was just solving for the specific problems I saw with minimum effort.

I would imagine there might be performance problems with just replacing with a percpu semaphore (e.g. with zoned).
Maybe it needs to be a RCU list? Or different arenas like memory allocators (since you are already using size classes...)?

Signals: performance concerns, alternative solutions
[PATCH 1/6] btrfs: skip unhelpful size class relaxing allocation loops Neutral llm-chunked-no-replies

This patch optimizes the Btrfs allocation loop by skipping unnecessary iterations when size class relaxation is not beneficial. It achieves this by tracking whether a block group was skipped due to its size class and only running loops that are relevant. This change aims to improve performance by reducing the number of unnecessary allocations. The approach taken involves modifying the `find_free_extent_update_loop` function to check for the `size_class_skipped` flag before running certain loops, and updating the `find_free_extent` function to set this flag when a block group is skipped due to its size class.

[PATCH 2/6] btrfs: don't take block group references in find_free_extent() Neutral llm-chunked-no-replies

This patch optimizes the find_free_extent() function in Btrfs by removing unnecessary get/put pairs on block group references. The loop in this function is already protected by a semaphore, making it impossible for the reference count to be the last one. This change saves time on filesystems with many full block groups and reduces the overhead of refcounting.

[PATCH 3/6] btrfs: remove redundant checks in find_free_extent() hint path Neutral llm-chunked-no-replies

This patch removes redundant checks in the find_free_extent() function's hint path, making it more efficient by only checking for the unique condition that applies to hints. The hint is a long-lasting reference to a block group and must be checked if it's still attached to the list of block groups. Other checks are not specific to hints and can be shared with normally reached block groups. This change improves performance without compromising data integrity.

[PATCH 4/6] btrfs: factor the find_free_extent() block group skip into a helper Neutral llm-chunked-no-replies

This patch refactors a complex block of code in the btrfs find_free_extent() function into a separate helper function called find_free_extent_skip_block_group(). This helper function checks if a block group can be skipped during the search for free extents, and returns true if it should be skipped. The original code has been simplified by removing duplicated logic and making it easier to understand.

[PATCH 5/6] btrfs: find_free_extent checks full block groups unlocked Neutral llm-chunked-no-replies

This patch optimizes the find_free_extent function in Btrfs by skipping block groups that are full and cannot satisfy an allocation request without taking a lock. This is done because checking if a block group can satisfy an allocation does not guarantee that it will be able to do so when locked, and racing with a free operation would result in the same outcome. The patch improves performance on large filesystems with many full block groups by reducing the number of unnecessary checks. Performance measurements show significant improvements in sequential 1G fallocates and sync operations after creating new files.

[PATCH 6/6] btrfs: find_free_extent checks size class unlocked Neutral llm-chunked-no-replies

This patch modifies the find_free_extent function in Btrfs to skip block groups that have an incorrect size class without acquiring a lock on them. This change is intended to improve performance by reducing the number of locks taken, especially when dealing with large file systems containing thousands of block groups. The patch also includes benchmark results showing improved performance after applying this modification.

Ongoing Submitted 2026-09-22 Last comment 2026-09-28 ⏱ No recent activity [PATCH 0/2] btrfs: ENOSPC fixes for unused block groups Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch series addresses ENOSPC (no space left on device) errors caused by unused block groups in a Btrfs filesystem. The patches aim to prevent these errors by allocating system chunk space earlier and committing deletions of unused block groups more aggressively when the system is low on space.

Signals: confusion, uncertainty
Boris Burkov (author) ↳ replying to Filipe Manana Neutral LLM
The author acknowledged that the condition for checking low system space is confusing and explained its subtlety, suggesting to rename functions and differentiate between two conditions to improve clarity.
Show original comment 
It is a bit confusing and I am not sure how to make it as nice as
possible. So the condition is:

  low = left < bytes || info->total_bytes < system_space_needed(info)
  ...
  if (low) {
  ...
  }

So ret != 0 implies low is true, but not necessarily left < bytes.
Basically the idea is that if the chunk allocation designed to protect
the chunk space in the future fails but we still have enough for right
now (ret && left >= bytes) then we can still add bytes to the block_rsv.

Would it help if I tried to give separate names to the two conditions
and did something like:

needs_space = left < bytes
wants_space = total < system_space_target()
low = needs_space || wants_space

...

if (!ret || !needs_space) {
 ...
}

So rename system_space_needed() to system_space_target() or something
like that, and then differentiate the minimum to add to the block_rsv
more clearly from the desire to add a system chunk? The current
system_space_needed() is kinda misleaded cause with writeback re-cowing,
we might use more than that in a txn anyway so it is not a hard
guarantee.

Thanks for the review,
Boris
View on lore ↗
Signals: acknowledged a complexity issue, suggested improvement
Ongoing Submitted 2026-09-22 Last comment 2026-09-28 ⏱ No recent activity [PATCH 1/2] btrfs: allocate additional SYSTEM space earlier Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch addresses a performance issue in Btrfs file systems where large block devices can accumulate thousands of system chunks, leading to slow deletion of unused block groups when the file system is filled up and then freed en-masse. The problem arises because the current implementation allocates new system chunks only when the space left is smaller than the reservation for a single chunk tree update. To fix this, the patch introduces a new function `system_space_needed` that estimates the upper bound on bytes needed to modify every leaf in the chunk tree and allocates additional system space earlier, ensuring that all block groups can be deleted in one transaction.

Ongoing Submitted 2026-09-22 ⏱ No comments yet [PATCH 2/2] btrfs: commit after deleting an unused block group if system space is low Neutral llm-chunked-no-replies

This patch addresses a problem where deleting multiple unused block groups in one transaction can fail due to insufficient system space, resulting in an -ENOSPC error. To solve this issue, the patch introduces a mechanism that checks for low system space and commits a single transaction to delete a single block group at a time. This approach allows the system to recover unallocated space and improves the chances of successfully allocating system chunks. The patch modifies the btrfs_delete_unused_bgs function in fs/btrfs/block-group.c to implement this new behavior.

Last comment 2026-09-29 [PATCH v2 0/2] btrfs: ENOSPC fixes for unused block groups Neutral LLM

This patch series addresses ENOSPC issues in Btrfs by allocating system space earlier and eagerly committing deletions when the system chunk is at risk, thereby preventing allocation failures caused by unused block groups.

1 reviewer comment — 0 participants

[PATCH v2 1/2] btrfs: allocate additional SYSTEM space earlier Neutral llm-chunked-no-replies

This patch allocates additional system space earlier to prevent a large number of block groups from being deleted simultaneously, which can lead to ENOSPC errors. The current behavior reserves system chunks only when the space left is smaller than the reservation for a single chunk tree update, leading to tens of thousands of chunks being accumulated on very large file systems. To fix this, the patch introduces a new function `system_space_target` that computes a rough bound on the bytes needed to modify every leaf in the chunk tree and allocates system space when it is half full. This change should only affect very large file systems.

[PATCH v2 2/2] btrfs: commit after deleting an unused block group if system space is low Neutral llm-chunked-no-replies

This patch addresses a problem where deleting multiple unused block groups in one transaction fails due to lack of system space, resulting in an -ENOSPC error. To mitigate this issue, the patch introduces a mechanism that commits a single transaction after deleting a single unused block group when system space is low. This approach ensures that freed space is available for subsequent allocations, making it more likely to succeed even with limited system space.

> If you look into the free space cache inside each block group, it does
> in fact index by gap size as well as gap offset.
>
> However if you do that at the block group level, then each allocation
> requires a write to the global ordering of block groups so that means
> having many parallel allocating threads now requires them to fight over
> a write lock on that ordered structure. I think that at least rules out
> naive solutions.
>
> You could probably resolve it with some more clever scheme like batching
> or queuing the work to update the ordering, or maybe some duplicated
> view with atomic cut overs.
>
> If you are interested in allocator performance, another thing we should
> consider is resurrecting more of
> https://lore.kernel.org/linux-btrfs/0102019bb2ff5805-8aa151b8-1fe7-4087-9610-4c3314b3b144-000000@eu-west-1.amazonses.com/
>
> I actually didn't realize that Martin had hit a few of the same things
> in that series so now I feel a bit bad about not working harder to
> incorporate his work earlier.
>
> If this series of mine goes in, we are ok on the put/get part since I
> just got rid of it, and I was planning to get rid of data_rwsem after
> https://lore.kernel.org/linux-btrfs/20260916-btrfs-remove-v1-space-cache-v3-0-10ebde03b96f@columbia.edu/
> goes in. But I have not tried to tackle the scalability of groups_sem
> yet, and would probably start by seriously evaluating Martin's patch and
> re-sending his work for that one.

Np, I was just solving for the specific problems I saw with minimum effort.

I would imagine there might be performance problems with just replacing with a percpu semaphore (e.g. with zoned).
Maybe it needs to be a RCU list? Or different arenas like memory allocators (since you are already using size classes...)?

That's the solution I tried before, it turns out it's more complex to 
get all the parsing and validation done.

Meanwhile we only need 4 slots to handle, thus this 4 options method is 
surprisingly easier to implement.

Thanks,
Qu

> 
>>
>> The number 0 means the newest slot, which should be the same as the one
>> recorded in the super block and no need to load any backup root.
>>
>> 1/2/3 means the second/third/fourth(oldest) backup slot to use.
>>
>> The existing "rescue=usebackuproot" is just an alias of
>> "rescue=usebackuproot_1".
>>
>> This will allow users to choose which backup root to use, thus can have
>> a better control than blindly choose any slot that can pass the very
>> basic root node checks.

Last comment 2026-10-02 [PATCH] mm/damon: use damon_get_monitor_folio() for hugetlb entries Neutral LLM

This patch refactors the acquisition of hugetlb folios in DAMON to use a single helper function, damon_get_monitor_folio(), which is already used for monitored LRU and hugetlb folios. This change simplifies the code and ensures consistency across different paths.

1 reviewer comment — 0 participants
0 participants
SJ Park Reviewed-by Positive LLM
Approved the patch and mentioned that it will be picked up by Andrew Morton if not added to mm.git within a week.
Show original comment 
Hello Gregory,

On Fri,  2 Oct 2026 10:07:27 -0400 "Gregory Price (Meta)" <gourry@gourry.net> wrote:

> From: Gregory Price <gourry@gourry.net>
> 
> Hugetlb folio acquisition is open-coded in three places:
>   - damon_hugetlb_mkold()
>   - damon_young_hugetlb_entry()
>   - damon_va_probe_hugetlb_entry()
> 
> DAMON already has damon_get_monitor_folio(), which paddr uses to
> get monitored LRU and hugetlb folios and to filter them through
> damon_folio_acceptable().  These paths bypass that check.

[PATCH v4 0/7] sched/numa: stop VMA scan filters from gating promotion 7 patches
Show 7 individual patches
  • [1/7] mm: support promotion-only NUMA hinting scans
  • [2/7] mm: allow shared folios to be promoted to a fast tier
  • [3/7] sched/numa: scan read-only file mappings in tiering mode
  • [4/7] sched/numa: separate VMA placement from scan continuation
  • [5/7] sched/numa: scan PID-inactive VMAs for promotion
  • [6/7] mm: use BIT() for change_protection() flags
  • [7/7] mm: use VMA flag helpers in NUMA balancing
Neutral llm-chunked-no-replies

This patch series addresses poor NUMA balancing behavior in a production deployment by stopping VMA scan filters from gating memory-tier promotion. The issue arises because the global balancing mode cannot describe the intent of an individual VMA/PTE walk (placement vs promotion). To fix this, the patch plumbs scan reasoning into the prot_none injection while retaining placement-scan filtering. It introduces promotion-only NUMA protection walks and derives private VMA state in the folio eligibility check. The patches also separate VMA placement eligibility from partial-scan continuation and scan PID-inactive VMAs for promotion. Additionally, it uses BIT() for change_protection flags and the VMA flag API in touched NUMA-balancing code.

Last comment 2026-09-30 Re: [PATCH] mm/numa_balancing: allow migrate on protnone reference with MPOL_WEIGHTED_INTERLEAVE policy Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch removes a limitation in the NUMA balancing code that prevents migration of pages when the policy has MPOL_WEIGHTED_INTERLEAVE set and there is no preferred node (protnone reference). The change allows for more flexible page placement, potentially improving performance by enabling NUMA balancing to optimize placement among multiple nodes.

5 reviewer comments — 0 participants
0 participants
Li Zhe ↳ replying to Gregory Price Reviewed-by Neutral LLM
Reviewer Li Zhe noted that the patch only allows migrate on protnone reference for MPOL_WEIGHTED_INTERLEAVE policy, and questioned why not also allow it for MPOL_INTERLEAVE policy, but agreed that allowing it for both policies is reasonable and will be implemented in v2.
Show original comment 
Thanks for pointing this out.

I focused on MPOL_WEIGHTED_INTERLEAVE in v1 because the motivating use
case is to seed memory across tiers with a configurable ratio at
allocation time, and then let NUMA balancing/memory tiering adjust the
placement based on access patterns. With equal weights,
MPOL_WEIGHTED_INTERLEAVE can also cover the regular interleave
allocation pattern.

That said, I agree that MPOL_INTERLEAVE can be handled consistently as
well. Since this is still an explicit opt-in via MPOL_F_NUMA_BALANCING,
unless others see a reason to keep MPOL_INTERLEAVE out, I will extend
this in v2 to cover both MPOL_INTERLEAVE and MPOL_WEIGHTED_INTERLEAVE
with the same nodemask constraint.

Thanks,
Zhe

Signals: requested changes
Li Zhe ↳ replying to Gregory Price Reviewed-by Needs Work LLM
reviewer suggested reordering the logic to handle MPOL_F_MORON before policy-specific cases, allowing for a shared migrate-on-fault path among policies that opt in to this behavior
Show original comment 
Yes, that makes sense to me.

I do not see a reason to keep the MPOL_F_MORON handling limited to
specific policy cases. The flag already means that migrate-on-fault
placement should target the accessing CPU's node, and the nodemask check
is the common constraint we want for all policies that opt in to this
behavior.

I will rework v2 to handle MPOL_F_MORON before the policy-specific
switch. Then the switch can remain responsible for the non-MPOL_F_MORON
misplaced logic, while MPOL_BIND, MPOL_PREFERRED_MANY, MPOL_INTERLEAVE
and MPOL_WEIGHTED_INTERLEAVE can share the same migrate-on-fault path.

Thanks,
Zhe
View on lore ↗
Signals: requested changes
Gregory Price (author) ↳ replying to Li Zhe Neutral LLM
Author acknowledged Li Zhe's suggestion to remove the F_MORON check and instead proposed breaking up the change into separate parts, considering different behavior for task-mempolicy and vma-mempolicy, and limiting the change based on NUMA balancing mode. No fix is planned in this response.
Show original comment 
I do think it's worth breaking this up a bit, we can decide whether we
want to apply it to interleave separate of the larger change.

there is also slightly different behavior for task-mempolicy and
vma-mempolicy, because vma-mempolicy applies interleave via index while
task mempolicy does it based on a rolling counter, so you'll want to
think about what happens on repeated faults

i.e.

1) fault in a VMA interleaved based on idx
2) a bunch of tiering and pageout/swap happens
3) we fault a page back in based on idx - that means this page is
   forever-faulted onto that location rather than taking that as an
   indication that maybe it should be local

Maybe we're ok with that, but we should probably think about it a bit.

We may also want to limit this based on NUMA balancing being in tiering
mode vs normal mode.  This only makes sense in tiering mode, in my
opinion.  In normal mode i'm not sure it makes as much sense - and
that's probably where this all came from.

tl;dr: If we want to change this behavior for interleave, we should
probably give more thought for how it should apply more generally
instead of just hacking on support to one or two modes.

~Gregory

The patch proposes to allow migration on protnone reference with MPOL_WEIGHTED_INTERLEAVE policy, but reviewers raise concerns about consistency and the need for additional flags.

2 reviewer comments — 0 participants
0 participants
David Hildenbrand (Arm) ↳ replying to Gregory Price Needs Work LLM
Raised concerns about inconsistent semantics and the need for additional flags to honor weights in weighted interleave policy. Suggested that setting these semantics in stone would make it difficult to change them later.
Signals: inconsistent semantics, need for additional flags
Gregory Price Neutral LLM
Proposed that migration on protnone reference with MPOL_WEIGHTED_INTERLEAVE policy should be allowed, but acknowledged potential inconsistencies and limitations. Suggested that the distinction may only make sense in TIERING mode.
Show original comment 
On Wed, Sep 30, 2026 at 02:32:16PM +0200, David Hildenbrand (Arm) wrote:
> > 
> > Right, but you're requesting this configuration.  If you didn't set
> > F_NUMA_BALANCING then you get the existing semantics.
> > 
> > In the existing semantics, if we're INTERLEAVE and WEIGHTED_INTERLEAVE
> > we just always say "no tiering for you".
> > 
> > But as an opt-in option?  I don't quite see the argument for saying
> > interleaved regions (or tasks) to be opted-out if the user asks for it -
> > that just seems like an arbitrary limitation.
> 
> Just to be clear, what I am saying is: the proposed semantics are inconsistent
> (weighted when mechanism A honors them and mechanism B doesn't honor them), but
> once we set these semantics in stone like that, we cannot easily change them
> later because some user might depend on that behavior.
> 
> So you'll need yet another flag to say "NUMA balancing really also honors the
> weights". And that's where it all gets ugly.
> 

I would agree with you if demotion didn't entirely ignoring mempolicy.

On a tiered system, mempolicy *only* applies to initial (or fault-in)
placement, and otherwise is more or less entirely ignored.  This is
a case where it's not ignored - which is actually inconsistent with
the rest of the tiering tools (demotion, damon).

This is why I said this distinction may only make sense in TIERING
mode, while in NORMAL mode you likely just want to fault it back to
its original interleave location.

Also file interleave (indexed by offset) vs task (counter-incremented)
policies are affected by these changes very differently, which I asked
for some thought on.

As tiering develops, the less I think task-mempolicy as a whole makes
much sense (because it's at-best advisory).

~Gregory

Patch proposes to allow migration of pages on protnone reference with MPOL_WEIGHTED_INTERLEAVE policy, aiming to improve initial faulting placement and reduce hot memory consolidation.

2 reviewer comments — 0 participants

Signals: NEEDS_WORK
Zi Yan ↳ replying to Gregory Price Positive LLM
Proposed an alternative solution where userspace can switch to numa balancing via set_mempolicy() or mbind() after a certain threshold is met.
Show original comment 
On 30 Sep 2026, at 10:50, Gregory Price wrote:

> On Wed, Sep 30, 2026 at 10:02:23PM +0800, Li Zhe wrote:
>> The use case is closer to the second one, but the main motivation is not
>> only startup-time performance. The more important point is that each
>> workload has its own DDR/CXL budget assigned by the workload manager.
>>
>
> mempolicy is the wrong interface to do budget policy, you'd be better
> off looking at Joshua's memcg tiered node solutions for that.
>
>> MPOL_WEIGHTED_INTERLEAVE is useful because it lets new allocations
>> follow that per-workload budget from the beginning, instead of placing
>> everything on one tier first and correcting the placement later. This is
>> important for workload orchestration because the workload starts from a
>> placement close to its assigned DDR/CXL ratio.
>>
>
> This however is reasonable to me - you'd prefer to spread out the cost
> of initial faulting placement explicitly, rather than simply take
> fallbacks when the top-tier budget becomes pressured.
>
> i.e. w/o interleave:
>
> [node 0       ]
> ^^^^^^^^^^^^^^ alloc until full
>                 vvvvvvvvv fallback
>                 [node 1          ]
>
> in this scenario you end up with considerable hot memory
> on the remote node consolidated in time-space (everything
> allocated after node0 becomes full skews heavily toward
> node1)
>
> Tiering then likely takes many faults to rebalance after
> you've already reached node0 limits.
>
> w/ interleave
>
> [node 0                                      ]
>  ^^^vv^^^vv^^^vv^^^vv^^^vv^^^vv....
> [node 1                                      ]
>
> In this scenario you do an initial fill distributed by weight
> and then let tiering figure it out without consolidating all
> of the pressure to the point where node0 has no space left.
>
> That said - this seems like mostly an initial-fill problem, after
> you initially fill your memory, a new allocation largely implies
> the memory is hot - and you probably prefer that to be local.

If this is a initial-fill problem, can userspace set weighted interleave
initially? The program or harness can observe memory usage of the program
or related NUMA nodes and switch the policy to numa balancing via
set_mempolicy() or mbind() without MPOL_MF_MOVE after certain threshold
is met?



Best Regards,
Yan, Zi

Joanne Koong

7 items
Patches Submitted (2)
[PATCH v1 0/3] mm/readahead: avoid per-folio memcg reclaim 3 patches
Show 3 individual patches
  • [1/3] mm: memcontrol: factor reclaim logic out of try_charge_memcg()
  • [2/3] mm: memcontrol: add mem_cgroup_reclaim_for_batch()
  • [3/3] mm/readahead: avoid per-folio memcg reclaim
Neutral llm-chunked-no-replies

This patch series addresses a performance issue in Linux's readahead feature by avoiding per-folio memory cgroup (memcg) reclaim. When multiple tasks fault in the same cgroup, the frequent per-folio charges trigger reclaim, leading to livelocks and high CPU usage. The patches modify readahead to charge all folios in its window at once when the memcg is at its limit, reducing reclaim passes by 94-96% and eliminating livelocks on a test system with btrfs file systems and compress-force=zstd:3.

Ongoing Submitted 2026-09-16 Last comment 2026-09-25 ⏱ No recent activity [PATCH v2 0/3] mm: split underused anonymous mTHP folios 3 patches
Show 3 individual patches
  • [1/3] mm/huge_memory: make thp_underused() work for mTHP folios Submitted 2026-09-16
  • [2/3] mm/huge_memory: don't queue folios that can never be underused Submitted 2026-09-16
  • [3/3] mm/memory: add anonymous mTHP folios to the deferred split list Submitted 2026-09-16
Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch series addresses memory regressions caused by enabling 2M THP=always on arm64 with 64k base pages. The patches split underused anonymous mTHP folios, which are not currently reclaimed under memory pressure. Patch 1 makes the thp_underused() function size-aware to accommodate larger folio sizes. Patch 2 optimizes existing behavior by keeping folios that can never be found underused off the deferred split queue. Patch 3 queues anonymous mTHP folios from map_anon_folio_pte_nopf(), mirroring what is already done for PMD folios. The patches aim to provide a solution for deploying 2M THP=always on arm64 without causing memory regressions.

7 reviewer comments — 0 participants
0 participants
Lance Yang Reviewed-by Needs Work LLM
Lance Yang noted that the use of an absolute value for khugepaged_max_ptes_none as both the mTHP underused threshold and the PMD collapse threshold is inconsistent, leading to different values being used depending on whether it's applied in mTHP or PMD context. He suggested using a per-order value instead, which would also allow preserving the current behavior by leaving max_ptes_none unset.
Show original comment 
On Wed, Sep 16, 2026 at 03:44:34PM -0700, Joanne Koong wrote:
>PMD-sized THPs that are mostly zero-filled are reclaimed under memory
>pressure by the deferred split shrinker, but this is not done for mTHP
>folios. At Meta we would like to deploy 2M THP=always on arm64 with 64k
>base pages, as 2M provides the contpte benefits while the PMD size there
>(512M) is too big to use. However, 2M THP=always causes memory regressions
>unless the unused portions of those folios can be broken down and
>reclaimed.
>
>v1 did two things. It scaled khugepaged_max_ptes_none down per folio order and
>it queued every anonymous mTHP folio. David objected to the scaling since
>collapse had already rejected proportional scaling as either letting the memory
>footprint creep or being confusing to reason about. David and Barry separately
>objected to the queuing, which adds list_lru lock contention on every anonymous
>fault, and hurts lower-order use cases such as Android's order-2-only
>configuration. Johannes suggested keeping khugepaged_max_ptes_none absolute and
>simply not queuing folios that could never exceed it, which addresses both.
>
>Read as an absolute count, khugepaged_max_ptes_none also implies the
>smallest folio that takes part in underused splitting. A 2M folio on 64k
>base pages is 32 pages, so the knob has to be set below 32 for those to
>be queued at all, and any folio with no more pages than the value stays off
>the queue entirely.

Em ... I'm not sure max_ptes_none should also become the mTHP underused
threshold ...

Say we're on arm64 with 64K pages and set it to 16. Khugepaged PMD
collapse uses 16, while mTHP collapse turns the same value into 0. With
this series, an order-5 (2 MiB) mTHP enters the deferred split queue
because it has 32 pages, and is considered underused once more than 16
pages are zero-filled. So the same knob means 16, 0, and 16 depending on
where it is used ... That's a bit odd ... no?

Would a per-order value make more sense here?

An unset value could preserve the current behavior, while an explicit
value could control both mTHP collapse and underused splitting. That
would also give us a way to leave max_ptes_none as the legacy fallback
instead of adding more meaning to it :)

Cheers, Lance

>Patch 1 makes thp_underused() folio-size aware. Today it only ever sees
>PMD-sized folios, so it assumes HPAGE_PMD_NR pages throughout. Patch 3
>breaks that assumption, so patch 1 generalizes it first. No functional
>changes are introduced.
>
>Patch 2 keeps folios that can never be found underused off the deferred
>split queue, as suggested by Johannes. This changes/optimizes existing
>behavior. With the default khugepaged/max_ptes_none, PMD folios are queued
>today and then dropped again by the first scan without ever having been
>splittable. After this patch they are not queued at all.
>
>Patch 3 queues anonymous mTHP folios from map_anon_folio_pte_nopf(),
>mirroring what map_anon_folio_pmd_nopf() already does for PMD folios. This
>covers both the fault path and the khugepaged mTHP collapse path.
>
>One consequence of keeping the knob absolute is that it is shared with
>collapse. A value low enough to be useful for 2M mTHP is a tiny
>fraction of a 512M PMD, so khugepaged will only collapse to PMD order
>when the region is almost fully populated. That is fine for the deployment
>this series targets, which does not use PMD THP on arm64, but anyone wanting
>both PMD THP and 2M mTHP on 64k pages should be aware of it.
>
>Thanks,
>Joanne
>
>Changelog
>---------
>v1: https://lore.kernel.org/linux-mm/20260707201735.4113107-1-joannelkoong@gmail.com/
>
>Changes since v1:
>* Drop the per-order scaling of khugepaged_max_ptes_none. Keep it
>  absolute, matching collapse (David, Johannes)
>* New patch 2 (suggested by Johannes): don't queue folios that can never
>  be underused, which both bounds what patch 3 adds and stops queuing PMD
>  folios under default settings (Johannes, David, Barry)
>* Fix the thp_underused() early exit to scale to the folio's own size
>
>Joanne Koong (3):
>  mm/huge_memory: make thp_underused() work for mTHP folios

> With scaling, I don't see a way of avoiding having to queue every

Hi Joanne,

you may take a look at Shivank's report:
https://lore.kernel.org/linux-mm/08f63301a58f0e0c38480553295ad9af95928da6.camel@amd.com/

It's not the same lock, but the problem could be quite similar to the
`lru_cache` case.

The `lru_cache` work for mTHP order-2 not only resolves AMD's
regression after enabling order-2 mTHP, but also improves overall
performance. before that, there was a regression after enabling mTHP
due to lru lock contention:

"
Performance
===========

                  Base       Patched    delta
4K                109.60     109.11     -0.4%
mTHP-16K          144.10      99.00    -31.3%
mTHP-64K          104.75     104.25     -0.5%

mTHP-16K was ~31% slower than THP-never, due to lock contentions.
With your series, mTHP-16 performs ~9% faster than THP-never.
"

>
> For b), v1 unconditionally queued every folio of order >= 2. v2 added
> an optimization patch for skipping queueing entirely if max_ptes_none
> is at its default value, which would also carry over if we went with
> scaling. So I think with that patch added, for the objection to b), it
> depends on what max_ptes_none Android actually runs. At the default
> max_ptes_none, nothing would be queued at all, so a config that sees
> no benefit from splitting small folios would pay nothing for it.

I assume your case is that you always use 2MB mTHP and don't use
512MB PMD-sized THP at all, so you don't have the conflict Lance
mentioned? For Android, I don't think we would enable both 16KB mTHP
and THP either.

But thinking about it again, for a general-purpose system, I also find
it a bit strange if we have both mTHP and THP enabled.

>
> Does that make scaling acceptable to you and Barry? If so, I'd prefer
> that as well because if/when intermediary values are supported for
> mTHP collapse, that support would have to be proportional, and it'd be
> easier to reconcile the two with split also scaled. If not, then what
> would be the suggestion for where to take v3?
>
> Happy to also discuss some of this at LPC in person, if that would be
> more helpful.

Thanks
Barry

---

> Kiryl, would your rework allow all values for mTHP collapse or would
> it stil only allow HPAGE_PMD_NR = 1 and 0?
> 
>> Would a per-order value make more sense here?
>>
> 
> I feel like per-order might be too many knobs? What about a percentage
> that might apply to all mTHP orders?

We discussed all that in the past, and concluded that we will have to support
the existing toggle. I played with using a percentage, but having two toggles
possibly mean the same thing (and some values not being possible) turned ugly.

So the conclusion for me was: no new toggles.

> 
>> An unset value could preserve the current behavior, while an explicit
>> value could control both mTHP collapse and underused splitting. That
> 
> Ah are you proposing that mTHP collapse can then take all values from 
> 0 to mTHP number of pages?
> I think one of the inital version of Nicos patches for mTHP collpase
> allowed that (hopefully I am not misremembering). Nico, what was the
> reason for only allowing 0 and max?

The creep problem was discussed plenty of times. That's when we decided to start
with 0 and max, and only add support for scaling later, once we actually know
which use cases would need them.  Without support for deferred shrinker, there
wasn't really the need to support other values.

Some of it can be had in:

commit 5a9c05d86683db5449b7fefd278c461cffb55234
Author: Nico Pache <nico.pache@linux.dev>
Date:   Fri Jun 5 10:14:11 2026 -0600

    mm/khugepaged: generalize __collapse_huge_page_* for mTHP support

    generalize the order of the __collapse_huge_page_* and collapse_max_*
    functions to support future mTHP collapse.

    The current mechanism for determining collapse with the
    khugepaged_max_ptes_none value is not designed with mTHP in mind.  This
    raises a key design issue: if we support user defined max_pte_none values
    (even those scaled by order), a collapse of a lower order can introduces
    an feedback loop, or "creep", when max_ptes_none is set to a value greater
    than HPAGE_PMD_NR / 2.  [1]

    With this configuration, a successful collapse to order N will populate
    enough pages to satisfy the collapse condition on order N+1 on the next
    scan.  This leads to unnecessary work and memory churn.

    To fix this issue introduce a helper function that will limit mTHP
    collapse support to two max_ptes_none values, 0 and HPAGE_PMD_NR - 1.
    This effectively supports two modes: [2]

    - max_ptes_none=0: never collapses if it encounters an empty PTE or a PTE
      that maps the shared zeropage. Consequently, no memory bloat.
    - max_ptes_none=511 (on 4k pagesz): Always collapse to the highest
      available mTHP order.

    This removes the possibility of "creep", and a warning will be emitted if
    any non-supported max_ptes_none value is configured with mTHP enabled.
    Any intermediate value will default mTHP collapse to max_ptes_none=0.

    mTHP collapse will not honor the khugepaged_max_ptes_shared or
    khugepaged_max_ptes_swap parameters, and will fail if it encounters a
    shared or swapped entry.


Tracing back the discussion on that patch should give you all the details.


-- 
Cheers,

David

---

actually be able to split some. Was there a good reason why that idea was dropped?

>>> reclaimed.
>>>
>>> v1 did two things. It scaled khugepaged_max_ptes_none down per folio order and
>>> it queued every anonymous mTHP folio. David objected to the scaling since
>>> collapse had already rejected proportional scaling as either letting the memory
>>> footprint creep or being confusing to reason about. David and Barry separately
>>> objected to the queuing, which adds list_lru lock contention on every anonymous
>>> fault, and hurts lower-order use cases such as Android's order-2-only
>>> configuration. Johannes suggested keeping khugepaged_max_ptes_none absolute and
>>> simply not queuing folios that could never exceed it, which addresses both.
>>>
>>> Read as an absolute count, khugepaged_max_ptes_none also implies the
>>> smallest folio that takes part in underused splitting. A 2M folio on 64k
>>> base pages is 32 pages, so the knob has to be set below 32 for those to
>>> be queued at all, and any folio with no more pages than the value stays off
>>> the queue entirely.
>>>
>>> Patch 1 makes thp_underused() folio-size aware. Today it only ever sees
>>> PMD-sized folios, so it assumes HPAGE_PMD_NR pages throughout. Patch 3
>>> breaks that assumption, so patch 1 generalizes it first. No functional
>>> changes are introduced.
>>>
>>> Patch 2 keeps folios that can never be found underused off the deferred
>>> split queue, as suggested by Johannes. This changes/optimizes existing
>>> behavior. With the default khugepaged/max_ptes_none, PMD folios are queued
>>> today and then dropped again by the first scan without ever having been
>>> splittable. After this patch they are not queued at all.
>>
>> I think we discussed queuing folios once the value changes and you would
>> actually be able to split some. Was there a good reason why that idea was dropped?
>>
> 
> Hi David,
> 
> I have a paragraph about this in the commit message of patch 2:
> 

Ah, okay, I only skimmed the cover letter so far.

On 9/23/26 02:44, Joanne Koong wrote:
> On Mon, Sep 21, 2026 at 3:12\u202fAM David Hildenbrand (Arm)
> <david@kernel.org> wrote:
>>
>> On 9/20/26 18:20, Lance Yang wrote:
>>>
>>
>> "David objected to the scaling", not quite. The tricky bit is the scaling when
>> collapsing (creep).
>>
>> When freeing, I'd assume scaling should be alright.
> 
> Ah, apologies for misinterpreting your comment.
> 
>>
>>>
>>> Em ... I'm not sure max_ptes_none should also become the mTHP underused
>>> threshold ...
>>>
>>> Say we're on arm64 with 64K pages and set it to 16. Khugepaged PMD
>>> collapse uses 16, while mTHP collapse turns the same value into 0. With
>>> this series, an order-5 (2 MiB) mTHP enters the deferred split queue
>>> because it has 32 pages, and is considered underused once more than 16
>>> pages are zero-filled. So the same knob means 16, 0, and 16 depending on
>>> where it is used ... That's a bit odd ... no?
>>
>> I very much prefer scaling over using absolute numbers as proposed here.
>>
> 
> With scaling, I don't see a way of avoiding having to queue every
> anonymous folio of order >=2 to the deferred split list at fault time
> in the non-default max_ptes_none case, since any folio can now
> potentially qualify as underused. 

Exactly.

I was primarily arguing that I don't want this overhead for the majority of
Linux installations out there that ship with

$ cat /sys/kernel/mm/transparent_hugepage/khugepaged/max_ptes_none
511

IOW: we add them (PMD THP folios today!) to the deferred list for
uuderused-scanning but never actually scan them because of:

	if (khugepaged_max_ptes_none == HPAGE_PMD_NR - 1)
		return false;

Now, what you are saying is we might want less overhead for installations that
modify max_ptes_none, like Meta's fleet. See below.

> This drew some objections in v1,
> particularly for the Android small folios case. I read that as ruling
> out scaling. The objections were a) the list_lru lock contention and

It was the "deferred shrinker list and lock" contention, yes.

> b) the limited upside vs cost of splitting small folios.
> 
> For a), I don't think there were any hard numbers showing this is a
> problem. If it would be helpful to have some benchmark numbers, I'm
> happy to run a synthetic one (patch 3 applied (which adds anon mTHP
> folios to the deferred split list), only order-2 enabled,
> max_ptes_none set low enough, all threads in one cgroup on one node so
> they contend on the same lock, each thread faulting in a large private
> anonymous mapping). I'm happy to run something else if anyone has a
> workload they would trust more. Johannes also mentioned batching as a
> mitigation. If the contention does turn out to be an issue, I'm happy
> to prototype that and measure it too.

Right, I raised that. Johannes thinks it could be fixed with batching. I was
concerned that it would become rather ugly. We'd have to see how that could look.

> 
> For b), v1 unconditionally queued every folio of order >= 2. v2 added
> an optimization patch for skipping queueing entirely if max_ptes_none
> is at its default value, which would also carry over if we went with
> scaling. So I think with that patch added, for the objection to b), it
> depends on what max_ptes_none Android actually runs. At the default
> max_ptes_none, nothing would be queued at all, so a config that sees
> no benefit from splitting small folios would pay nothing for it.

I would assume everybody except some special users that fine-tune underused
shrinker run with the default. I did some digging in the past regarding public
documentation about how to set the parameter, and most of them just defaulted to
511.

I would be surprised if Android even knows about this parameter ;)

> 
> Does that make scaling acceptable to you and Barry? If so, I'd prefer
> that as well because if/when intermediary values are supported for
> mTHP collapse, that support would have to be proportional, and it'd be
> easier to reconcile the two with split also scaled. If not, then what
> would be the suggestion for where to take v3?
My opinion is (open for discussion :) ) that scaling is likely the better
approach. With the following notes:

(1) If we want to enable mTHP collapse with scaling as well, this needs very
good documentation and also another thought on how to work around the problem of
creep (e.g., refusing to collapse if creep would be possible according to the
max_ptes_non setting and warning).

(2) Systems where the underused shrinker is effectively inactive should not add
folios to the deferred list. Including PMD THPs, which we unconditionally add today.

(3) If we want to exclude certain small folio sizes from the underused shrinker
(e.g., order-2? order-3?) it might be better to just hard-code that in the
kernel instead of giving the admin a choice it cannot possibly make easily.


-- 
Cheers,

David

Joanne Koong (author) Neutral LLM
Author addressed David's concern that the scaling of khugepaged_max_ptes_none should be alright when freeing, and acknowledged a misunderstanding in his previous comment about proportional scaling. Author also clarified that the queue has always been best-effort and will address requeueing eligible folios in a separate patch.

On Mon, Sep 21, 2026 at 3:23 AM David Hildenbrand (Arm)
<david@kernel.org> wrote:
>
> On 9/17/26 00:44, Joanne Koong wrote:
> > PMD-sized THPs that are mostly zero-filled are reclaimed under memory
> > pressure by the deferred split shrinker, but this is not done for mTHP
> > folios. At Meta we would like to deploy 2M THP=always on arm64 with 64k
> > base pages, as 2M provides the contpte benefits while the PMD size there
> > (512M) is too big to use. However, 2M THP=always causes memory regressions
> > unless the unused portions of those folios can be broken down and
> > reclaimed.
> >
> > v1 did two things. It scaled khugepaged_max_ptes_none down per folio order and
> > it queued every anonymous mTHP folio. David objected to the scaling since
> > collapse had already rejected proportional scaling as either letting the memory
> > footprint creep or being confusing to reason about. David and Barry separately
> > objected to the queuing, which adds list_lru lock contention on every anonymous
> > fault, and hurts lower-order use cases such as Android's order-2-only
> > configuration. Johannes suggested keeping khugepaged_max_ptes_none absolute and
> > simply not queuing folios that could never exceed it, which addresses both.
> >
> > Read as an absolute count, khugepaged_max_ptes_none also implies the
> > smallest folio that takes part in underused splitting. A 2M folio on 64k
> > base pages is 32 pages, so the knob has to be set below 32 for those to
> > be queued at all, and any folio with no more pages than the value stays off
> > the queue entirely.
> >
> > Patch 1 makes thp_underused() folio-size aware. Today it only ever sees
> > PMD-sized folios, so it assumes HPAGE_PMD_NR pages throughout. Patch 3
> > breaks that assumption, so patch 1 generalizes it first. No functional
> > changes are introduced.
> >
> > Patch 2 keeps folios that can never be found underused off the deferred
> > split queue, as suggested by Johannes. This changes/optimizes existing
> > behavior. With the default khugepaged/max_ptes_none, PMD folios are queued
> > today and then dropped again by the first scan without ever having been
> > splittable. After this patch they are not queued at all.
>
> I think we discussed queuing folios once the value changes and you would
> actually be able to split some. Was there a good reason why that idea was dropped?
>

Hi David,

I have a paragraph about this in the commit message of patch 2:

"Please note that the queue has always been best-effort. A folio that was
queued before khugepaged/max_ptes_none is lowered gets dropped from the
queue by the first scan that finds it's not underused, so changing the
sysctl has never retroactively applied to folios that were already
scanned. Requeueing eligible folios when the sysctl changes will be
addressed in a separate patch."

I will be sending out the change for this as its own separate patch.

Thanks,
Joanne

On Mon, Sep 21, 2026 at 3:12 AM David Hildenbrand (Arm)
<david@kernel.org> wrote:
>
> On 9/20/26 18:20, Lance Yang wrote:
> >
> > On Wed, Sep 16, 2026 at 03:44:34PM -0700, Joanne Koong wrote:
> >> PMD-sized THPs that are mostly zero-filled are reclaimed under memory
> >> pressure by the deferred split shrinker, but this is not done for mTHP
> >> folios. At Meta we would like to deploy 2M THP=always on arm64 with 64k
> >> base pages, as 2M provides the contpte benefits while the PMD size there
> >> (512M) is too big to use. However, 2M THP=always causes memory regressions
> >> unless the unused portions of those folios can be broken down and
> >> reclaimed.
> >>
> >> v1 did two things. It scaled khugepaged_max_ptes_none down per folio order and
> >> it queued every anonymous mTHP folio. David objected to the scaling since
>
> "David objected to the scaling", not quite. The tricky bit is the scaling when
> collapsing (creep).
>
> When freeing, I'd assume scaling should be alright.

Ah, apologies for misinterpreting your comment.

>
> >> collapse had already rejected proportional scaling as either letting the memory
> >> footprint creep or being confusing to reason about. David and Barry separately
> >> objected to the queuing, which adds list_lru lock contention on every anonymous
> >> fault, and hurts lower-order use cases such as Android's order-2-only
> >> configuration. Johannes suggested keeping khugepaged_max_ptes_none absolute and
> >> simply not queuing folios that could never exceed it, which addresses both.
> >>
> >> Read as an absolute count, khugepaged_max_ptes_none also implies the
> >> smallest folio that takes part in underused splitting. A 2M folio on 64k
> >> base pages is 32 pages, so the knob has to be set below 32 for those to
> >> be queued at all, and any folio with no more pages than the value stays off
> >> the queue entirely.
> >
> > Em ... I'm not sure max_ptes_none should also become the mTHP underused
> > threshold ...
> >
> > Say we're on arm64 with 64K pages and set it to 16. Khugepaged PMD
> > collapse uses 16, while mTHP collapse turns the same value into 0. With
> > this series, an order-5 (2 MiB) mTHP enters the deferred split queue
> > because it has 32 pages, and is considered underused once more than 16
> > pages are zero-filled. So the same knob means 16, 0, and 16 depending on
> > where it is used ... That's a bit odd ... no?
>
> I very much prefer scaling over using absolute numbers as proposed here.
>

With scaling, I don't see a way of avoiding having to queue every
anonymous folio of order >=2 to the deferred split list at fault time
in the non-default max_ptes_none case, since any folio can now
potentially qualify as underused. This drew some objections in v1,
particularly for the Android small folios case. I read that as ruling
out scaling. The objections were a) the list_lru lock contention and
b) the limited upside vs cost of splitting small folios.

For a), I don't think there were any hard numbers showing this is a
problem. If it would be helpful to have some benchmark numbers, I'm
happy to run a synthetic one (patch 3 applied (which adds anon mTHP
folios to the deferred split list), only order-2 enabled,
max_ptes_none set low enough, all threads in one cgroup on one node so
they contend on the same lock, each thread faulting in a large private
anonymous mapping). I'm happy to run something else if anyone has a
workload they would trust more. Johannes also mentioned batching as a
mitigation. If the contention does turn out to be an issue, I'm happy
to prototype that and measure it too.

For b), v1 unconditionally queued every folio of order >= 2. v2 added
an optimization patch for skipping queueing entirely if max_ptes_none
is at its default value, which would also carry over if we went with
scaling. So I think with that patch added, for the objection to b), it
depends on what max_ptes_none Android actually runs. At the default
max_ptes_none, nothing would be queued at all, so a config that sees
no benefit from splitting small folios would pay nothing for it.

Does that make scaling acceptable to you and Barry? If so, I'd prefer
that as well because if/when intermediary values are supported for
mTHP collapse, that support would have to be proportional, and it'd be
easier to reconcile the two with split also scaled. If not, then what
would be the suggestion for where to take v3?

Happy to also discuss some of this at LPC in person, if that would be
more helpful.

> >
> > Would a per-order value make more sense here?
> >
> > An unset value could preserve the current behavior, while an explicit
> > value could control both mTHP collapse and underused splitting. That
> > would also give us a way to leave max_ptes_none as the legacy fallback
> > instead of adding more meaning to it :)
>
> The problem is that: we don't want any new toggles unless unavoidable.

Thanks for your feedback, Lance - fwiw, it was my preference too as it
seemed least confusing :)
But I get the hesitation about adding something that can't be deleted.

Thanks,
Joanne

> you may take a look at Shivank's report:

> It's not the same lock, but the problem could be quite similar to the
> `lru_cache` case.
>
> The `lru_cache` work for mTHP order-2 not only resolves AMD's
> regression after enabling order-2 mTHP, but also improves overall
> performance. before that, there was a regression after enabling mTHP
> due to lru lock contention:
>
> "
> Performance
> ===========
>
>                   Base       Patched    delta
> 4K                109.60     109.11     -0.4%
> mTHP-16K          144.10      99.00    -31.3%
> mTHP-64K          104.75     104.25     -0.5%
>
> mTHP-16K was ~31% slower than THP-never, due to lock contentions.
> With your series, mTHP-16 performs ~9% faster than THP-never.
> "

Thanks for the link, Barry! Apologies for the late reply, I was out on PTO.

I ran your microbenchmark on my machine to see if I could reproduce
the results above and to see at what mTHP granularity it stops
degrading. My setup is a 2-socket Skylake, 40 cores / 80 threads,
threads free to run on all CPUs with memory bound to node 0 so they
all contend on one lruvec, running 80 threads x 16 MB x 200 loops.

These are the numbers I saw on the base (unpatched) version from
running perf lock contention on folio_lruvec_lock_irqsave ("lock wait"
is all 80 threads summed):

    size                 contentions   lock wait   folios/MB
    4K                       1075221      321.4 s         256
    mTHP-16K          4203612      392.8 s        64
    mTHP-32K          2064073      190.5 s        32
    mTHP-64K           212012        1.7 s          16
    mTHP-128K          117993        1.8 s           8
    mTHP-256K           64532        4.2 s           4
    mTHP-512K           32485        3.1 s           2
    mTHP-1024K           4313        0.3 s           1
    mTHP-2048K            725        0.1 s           0.5

I'm pretty much seeing the same results you mentioned above, where
mTHP-16k results in more lock contentions (though I didn't see a
runtime regression. I saw that 16k comes out a bit faster than 4k in
wall time), but 64k results in significantly less. For mTHP-32k, I see
less time overall spent waiting, but 2x the lock contention.

I re-ran the benchmark with the 3 patches in this series applied, with
the khugepaged/max_ptes_none sysfs value set to 0 so every folio gets
queued (the worst case) and measured the queueing cost by switching
shrink_underused on/off. I saw:

      size                 runtime delta between on/off   folios/MB
      mTHP-16K             +15.8%                                64
      mTHP-32K             +11.5%                                32
      mTHP-64K              +1.1%                                16
      mTHP-128K             +1.2%                                 8
      mTHP-256K             -0.6%                                  4
      mTHP-512K             +0.5%                                 2
      mTHP-1024K            -1.2%                                 1
      mTHP-2048K            -0.3%                              0.5

which mirrors the same pattern/trend as above (eg regresses at the 16k
and 32k cases, but not for 64k+).

I ran a perf analysis and the delta doesn't come from the deferred
split list's own lock, but actually from folio_lruvec_lock_irqsave:

      shrink_underused             0            1
      runtime                     6.00s           6.86s           +14.3%
      lruvec contentions     4206376      4234324      +0.7%
      lruvec wait            406.4 s           485.8 s         +19.5%
      avg wait                96.5us           114.5us
      list_lru_lock           absent            absent

The lruvec contentions count doesn't move much (0.7%) but the time
spent waiting goes up 19.5%. I think that's coming from the free path
rather than the fault path. In folios_put_refs(),
__page_cache_release() keeps the lruvec locked across the whole batch,
and from what I'm seeing, folio_unqueue_deferred_split() gets called
from inside that loop. I don't think your lru cache series helps this
directly since it batches on the add side but this cost is coming from
the free side inside a section that's already batched, but I think it
should help indirectly, since there would be less lruvec pressure /
contention overall. I'm not 100% sure about this, but think it might
be possible to move the unqueue from the deferred list to outside the
lruvec lock hold, though I need to look more into this.

For now though, I think it makes sense to have a hard-coded minimum
folio size for the underused shrinker, as you and David suggested.
Based on the results above, I think having 64k as the cut-off makes
sense.

>
> >
> > For b), v1 unconditionally queued every folio of order >= 2. v2 added
> > an optimization patch for skipping queueing entirely if max_ptes_none
> > is at its default value, which would also carry over if we went with
> > scaling. So I think with that patch added, for the objection to b), it
> > depends on what max_ptes_none Android actually runs. At the default
> > max_ptes_none, nothing would be queued at all, so a config that sees
> > no benefit from splitting small folios would pay nothing for it.
>
> I assume your case is that you always use 2MB mTHP and don't use
> 512MB PMD-sized THP at all, so you don't have the conflict Lance
> mentioned? For Android, I don't think we would enable both 16KB mTHP
> and THP either.

Thanks for the context on the Android case. For Meta's case, that is
correct, we are deploying 2M mTHP = always on ARM with 64k base pages,
with the 512M PMD size disabled.

Thanks,
Joanne

Signals: acknowledged misunderstanding, clarified existing behavior
Joanne Koong (author) Neutral LLM
The author is addressing David's concern about the scaling of khugepaged_max_ptes_none, which affects both PMD THP and mTHP folios. The author acknowledges that David was primarily arguing against adding overhead for most Linux installations, but agrees to add David's Suggested-by: tag in v3. The author also ran a benchmark to measure contention on the deferred split list lock, finding that it comes from the free path rather than enqueuing, and is considering moving the unqueue out of the loop where the lruvec lock is held.

>         if (khugepaged_max_ptes_none == HPAGE_PMD_NR - 1)
>                 return false;

Ah, I missed this nuance in your v1 reply - I thought it was objecting
to the queueing overhead for the non-default max_ptes_none case as
well. Patch 2 in this series does what you mentioned above and it
automatically applies to PMD THPs too. I'll add your Suggested-by: tag
to this in v3.

>
> Now, what you are saying is we might want less overhead for installations that
> modify max_ptes_none, like Meta's fleet. See below.
>
> > This drew some objections in v1,
> > particularly for the Android small folios case. I read that as ruling
> > out scaling. The objections were a) the list_lru lock contention and
>
> It was the "deferred shrinker list and lock" contention, yes.
>
> > b) the limited upside vs cost of splitting small folios.
> >
> > For a), I don't think there were any hard numbers showing this is a
> > problem. If it would be helpful to have some benchmark numbers, I'm
> > happy to run a synthetic one (patch 3 applied (which adds anon mTHP
> > folios to the deferred split list), only order-2 enabled,
> > max_ptes_none set low enough, all threads in one cgroup on one node so
> > they contend on the same lock, each thread faulting in a large private
> > anonymous mapping). I'm happy to run something else if anyone has a
> > workload they would trust more. Johannes also mentioned batching as a
> > mitigation. If the contention does turn out to be an issue, I'm happy
> > to prototype that and measure it too.
>
> Right, I raised that. Johannes thinks it could be fixed with batching. I was
> concerned that it would become rather ugly. We'd have to see how that could look.

I ran Barry's microbenchmark to get a sense of the contention (more of
the details are in my previous reply to Barry [1]). With max_ptes_none
set to 0 so that everything gets queued, and toggling shrink_underused
0 vs 1 with patch 3 applied, I saw roughly a 15.8% and 11.5%
performance hit on runtime at 16k and 32k, but only 1.1% at 64k and
noisy above that.

I didn't see the cost being from contention on the deferred list's
lock though. Perf showed that it was coming from
folio_lruvec_lock_irqsave, and I think that's from the free path where
in folios_put_refs(), __page_cache_release() keeps the lruvec locked
across the whole batch, and folio_unqueue_deferred_split() gets called
from inside that loop with that lruvec lock held. I don't think
batching the enqueues would help in that case, since the cost is
coming from the unqueue side and on a path that's already batched. I
think maybe we could move the unqueue out where the lruvec lock
doesn't need to be held while the unqueueing happens, but I need to
look more into that.

>
> >
> > For b), v1 unconditionally queued every folio of order >= 2. v2 added
> > an optimization patch for skipping queueing entirely if max_ptes_none
> > is at its default value, which would also carry over if we went with
> > scaling. So I think with that patch added, for the objection to b), it
> > depends on what max_ptes_none Android actually runs. At the default
> > max_ptes_none, nothing would be queued at all, so a config that sees
> > no benefit from splitting small folios would pay nothing for it.
>
> I would assume everybody except some special users that fine-tune underused
> shrinker run with the default. I did some digging in the past regarding public
> documentation about how to set the parameter, and most of them just defaulted to
> 511.
>
> I would be surprised if Android even knows about this parameter ;)
>
> >
> > Does that make scaling acceptable to you and Barry? If so, I'd prefer
> > that as well because if/when intermediary values are supported for
> > mTHP collapse, that support would have to be proportional, and it'd be
> > easier to reconcile the two with split also scaled. If not, then what
> > would be the suggestion for where to take v3?
> My opinion is (open for discussion :) ) that scaling is likely the better
> approach. With the following notes:
>
> (1) If we want to enable mTHP collapse with scaling as well, this needs very
> good documentation and also another thought on how to work around the problem of
> creep (e.g., refusing to collapse if creep would be possible according to the
> max_ptes_non setting and warning).

Agreed. And if/when collapse gains proportional support later on,
having split already scaled means one value governing both instead of
two.

>
> (2) Systems where the underused shrinker is effectively inactive should not add
> folios to the deferred list. Including PMD THPs, which we unconditionally add today.

Agreed.

>
> (3) If we want to exclude certain small folio sizes from the underused shrinker
> (e.g., order-2? order-3?) it might be better to just hard-code that in the
> kernel instead of giving the admin a choice it cannot possibly make easily.

Based off the benchmark results in [1] and [2], I think the cutoff
needs to be at 64k where we exclude anything under that. On 4k base
pages, this is order 4, but on 64k base pages, order 4 would mean 1M,
which I think would be overly conservative. I think it makes more
sense to express it as an absolute threshold value instead of by
order. I'll try to find an arm64 machine to double-check this on.

For v3, I'll go back to the scaling approach. I'll be traveling next
week and then will be at LPC after that, so my timeline is to submit
v3 after I come back from LPC.

Thanks,
Joanne

[1] https://lore.kernel.org/linux-mm/CAJnrk1a2PpUGTsCgS7jct2KET4K2zAL+_h+te8G1MaU9bXs99w@mail.gmail.com/
[2] https://lore.kernel.org/linux-mm/CAGsJ_4x_Q1Ku9r6zfC-K-o87juQ9JNQ8=OSNp+UZzW3MPtZXbQ@mail.gmail.com/

Reviewer Kiryl Shutsemau noted that the use of khugepaged_max_ptes_none as both the mTHP underused threshold and the PMD collapse threshold is inconsistent, resulting in different values depending on whether it's used for mTHP or PMD collapse. He suggested allowing proportional treatment of max_ptes_none for terminal mTHP size to address this issue.

I didn't change the policy here.

David Stevens ↳ replying to Peter Zijlstra Inline Review Needs Work LLM
Identified a potential issue with rdtsc() producing non-monotonic results on x86, which could explain the negative skew. Suggested adding a rbm() to match the behavior of rdtsc_ordered().
Show original comment 
On Wed, Sep 30, 2026 at 8:19 AM Peter Zijlstra <peterz@infradead.org> wrote:
>
> On Wed, Sep 30, 2026 at 10:22:52AM -0400, Johannes Weiner wrote:
> > On Tue, Sep 29, 2026 at 10:17:40AM +0200, Peter Zijlstra wrote:
> > > On Tue, Sep 29, 2026 at 10:10:04AM +0200, Peter Zijlstra wrote:
> > >
> > > > > When running browser workloads on an Intel N100 with a sustained
> > > > > psi.mem.some of 2-3%, this underflow is observed once every couple of
> > > > > hours.
> > >
> > > Reading is hard. This is sad, that's a relatively modern chip :-(
> >
> > I'm still struggling, but I'm under the weather so bear with me.
> >
> > That's a cpu_clock() implementation issue, right? "The value for
> > cpu_clock(i) is monotonic for constant i". Presumably violating that
> > would be a problem beyond psi?
>
> Yeah, although most everybody has a filter on top for some reason or
> another.
>
> Mostly it could happen that when the watchdog would detect the TSC
> wasn't stable after all, you'd be up a creek if you didn't also have
> this filter.
>
> But I'm very sad to see modern chips have shit TSC, that wasn't supposed
> to happen.

I ran artificial memory pressure tests on the other two devices I have
at hand - one with an Intel i5-1245U and one with an Intel Ultra 5
325. Both devices also observed negative elapsed durations. The
artificial workloads were much heavier and actually produced negative
elapsed durations every couple of minutes. So this doesn't look like a
platform-specific issue.

For reference, the negative elapsed durations I saw averaged a little
under 100ns. The largest duration I saw was 371ns.

If I'm following the code correctly, cpu_clock() on x86 ends up
calling rdtsc() via sched_clock(). The rdtsc() function is documented
to produce non-monotonic results when compared across CPUs, since the
rdtsc instruction can read the tsc out of order relative to other
instructions. If that results in get_recent_times() reading the tsc
before it reads the psi state, then I think that would explain the
negative skew. I added a rbm() immediately before the call to
cpu_clock() in get_recent_times() to match the behavior of
rdtsc_ordered(), and with that I didn't see any negative elapsed
durations during overnight testing. This does suggest that calling
through to sched_clock() might not provide strong enough guarantees on
x86 to satisfy the cpu_clock() contract.

-David

Joshua Hahn

Patches Submitted (2)
[PATCH v6 RESEND 0/5] mm/page_counter: move stock from mem_cgroup to page_counter 5 patches
Show 5 individual patches
  • [1/5] mm/memcontrol: flatten try_charge_memcg control flow
  • [2/5] mm/page_counter: introduce per-CPU stock
  • [3/5] mm/page_counter: make page_counter_try_charge() stock-aware
  • [4/5] mm/memcontrol: move memory stock to page counters
  • [5/5] mm/memcontrol: add stock to the memsw page counter
Neutral llm-chunked-no-replies

This patch moves the stock abstraction from mem_cgroup to page_counter, allowing each page_counter to have its own per-CPU cache of pre-charged pages. This change is intended to improve performance and scalability for future work on multiple page_counters in a single memcg. The existing seven-slot per-CPU design and drain policy are retained. For memcg v1 users, this decouples the memory and memsw stocks, potentially leading to different sizes of precharged cache for each. To maintain an invariant between these two stocks, the patch reports the larger value of usage_in_bytes for memsw reporting.

[PATCH 7.2.y] selftests/cgroup: account for zswap shrinker writeback Neutral llm-chunked-no-replies

This patch modifies the selftests/cgroup test to account for zswap shrinker writeback when checking if a cgroup triggers writeback for other cgroups. The test previously checked that the victim cgroup had zero writebacks, but this could lead to probabilistic failures due to delayed writebacks. Instead, the patch compares the writeback values before and after the aggressor runs and checks that the victim cgroup did not perform any additional writeback. This should fix systematic failure cases and make test failures less likely.

Re: [PATCH] mm/numa_balancing: allow migrate on protnone reference with MPOL_WEIGHTED_INTERLEAVE policy Neutral LLM
Last comment 2026-09-30 Re: [PATCH] mm/numa_balancing: allow migrate on protnone reference with MPOL_WEIGHTED_INTERLEAVE policy Neutral LLM

This patch removes a limitation in the NUMA balancing code that prevents migration of pages when the policy has MPOL_WEIGHTED_INTERLEAVE set and there is no preferred node (protnone reference). The change allows for more flexible page placement, potentially improving performance by enabling NUMA balancing to optimize placement among multiple nodes.

reviewer suggested reordering the logic to handle MPOL_F_MORON before policy-specific cases, allowing for a shared migrate-on-fault path among policies that opt in to this behavior
Show original comment 
Yes, that makes sense to me.

I do not see a reason to keep the MPOL_F_MORON handling limited to
specific policy cases. The flag already means that migrate-on-fault
placement should target the accessing CPU's node, and the nodemask check
is the common constraint we want for all policies that opt in to this
behavior.

I will rework v2 to handle MPOL_F_MORON before the policy-specific
switch. Then the switch can remain responsible for the non-MPOL_F_MORON
misplaced logic, while MPOL_BIND, MPOL_PREFERRED_MANY, MPOL_INTERLEAVE
and MPOL_WEIGHTED_INTERLEAVE can share the same migrate-on-fault path.

Gregory Price (author) ↳ replying to Li Zhe Neutral LLM
Author acknowledged Li Zhe's suggestion to remove the F_MORON check and instead proposed breaking up the change into separate parts, considering different behavior for task-mempolicy and vma-mempolicy, and limiting the change based on NUMA balancing mode. No fix is planned in this response.
Show original comment 
I do think it's worth breaking this up a bit, we can decide whether we
want to apply it to interleave separate of the larger change.

there is also slightly different behavior for task-mempolicy and
vma-mempolicy, because vma-mempolicy applies interleave via index while
task mempolicy does it based on a rolling counter, so you'll want to
think about what happens on repeated faults

i.e.

1) fault in a VMA interleaved based on idx
2) a bunch of tiering and pageout/swap happens
3) we fault a page back in based on idx - that means this page is
   forever-faulted onto that location rather than taking that as an
   indication that maybe it should be local

Maybe we're ok with that, but we should probably think about it a bit.

We may also want to limit this based on NUMA balancing being in tiering
mode vs normal mode.  This only makes sense in tiering mode, in my
opinion.  In normal mode i'm not sure it makes as much sense - and
that's probably where this all came from.

tl;dr: If we want to change this behavior for interleave, we should
probably give more thought for how it should apply more generally
instead of just hacking on support to one or two modes.

~Gregory

Patches Submitted (4)
Ongoing Submitted 2026-09-25 Last comment 2026-10-02 ⏱ No recent activity [PATCH v7 0/2] iommu/arm-smmu-v3: Make the queue depths tunable, and shrink them in a kdump kernel 2 patches
Show 2 individual patches
  • [1/2] iommu/arm-smmu-v3: Add a cmdq_max_n_shift module parameter Neutral Submitted 2026-09-25Last comment 2026-09-25 Nicolin Chen
  • [2/2] iommu/arm-smmu-v3: Default queue depths to one page in a kdump kernel Neutral Submitted 2026-09-25Last comment 2026-09-25 Breno Leitao Nicolin Chen
Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch series makes the queue depths in the ARM SMMUv3 IOMMU tunable, allowing them to be adjusted based on system requirements. The queues are currently sized from the IDR1 maximum and allocated at probe, which can consume megabytes of memory per queue per SMMU instance. In a kdump kernel, this can lead to out-of-memory errors due to limited crashkernel reservations. The patch series introduces a module parameter cmdq_max_n_shift that allows the command queue depth to be adjusted, and applies it in non-kdump kernels. This reduces the memory consumption of the queues from megabytes to kilobytes, allowing kdump kernels to save vmcores successfully.
